SOFTWARE / IT SERVICES 
 
 
SaaS Smash: is the SaaSpocalypse 
over or just pausing 
 
WHY YOU SHOULD READ THIS REPORT 
16 SEPTEMBER 2026 
Sector Research Report 
Production time: 16:36* (London time) 
AI Winner or AI Loser? When doubts over an asset’s long-
term viability arise, volatility spikes. When the asset is 
infamously ‘long duration’, like Software, volatility turns to 
blind panic. This crescendo peaked in Q1 with a >50% de-
rating and implied terminal values written off to zero.  
Today, select Software vendors are fighting back; with AI 
solutions hitting billion $ run rates, markets respond well.  
Now is the time to back the AI Winners (SAP, Sage, OVH 
(- to +)), avoid the AI Losers (DS (= to -), Planisware) and 
beware those yet to run the AI Fear Index gauntlet. 
Vertical software could be next as Frontier Labs and start-
ups take aim. We also highlight emerging Software AI 
winners while still at attractive entry points. 
Research Analysts & Publishing Entities 
Ben Castillo-Bernaus 
BNP Paribas London Branch 
Ines Mao 
BNP Paribas London Branch 
Thomas Poutrieux

SaaS Smash: is the SaaSpocalypse over or just pausing  
 
16 SEPTEMBER 2026 
Sector Research Report 
Production time: 16:36* (London time) 
Research Analysts & Publishing Entities 
Much has happened in the year since OpenAI’s GPT-5 launch triggered the ‘SaaSpocalypse’; 
investor fear peaked (Q1’26) and now early signs of AI Winner / Loser status are emerging. With 
the sector bouncing ~30% in recent weeks we take a temperature check: which companies should 
investors be chasing from here, and which should they use the recent rally to leave behind.  
Engineering Software could be next to suffer the ‘AI Fear Index’ peaking 
Cross-domain Software (‘Horizontal’) has passed ‘peak AI fear’; indiscriminate de-rating was most 
acute here among all Software subsegments (>50%). Now Winners / Losers are emerging, 
investors should back the ‘5 S’ Software Winners, even after the recent rally: in Europe we prefer 
Sage, SAP and Globally we also prefer ServiceNow, Salesforce, Snowflake. We think domain-
specific Software (‘Vertical’) could be next to face the AI Fear Index: multiples have been more 
insulated thanks to perceived deeper competitive moats, but AI challengers (Frontier Labs and AI 
native start-ups) are on the horizon with innovation aplenty. We unpack in detail where and how 
this could materialise.   
Most / least preferred in Europe. We upgrade OVH (+) / downgrade DSY (-) 
In European Software & IT Services we prefer Sage and SAP (both +) as best placed to emerge 
as Software AI Winners. We now believe OVH can become an AI Infrastructure Winner (upgraded 
to +), while Capgemini / Amadeus / Nemetschek / SoftwareOne (all +) offer attractive fundamentals 
at attractive valuations. We are less convinced Alten (recently downgraded to =) / TeamViewer (=) 
/ Sopra Steria (=) can overcome the market’s demanding ‘AI burden of proof’ or have enough 
operational upside, though we note valuations here are particularly dislocated. Hexagon / Ionos / 
Temenos (all =) have plenty to like, but performance ytd and valuation leaves little room for upside 
here. Our least preferred are Dassault Systèmes / Planisware (both -) screening unfavourably on 
the AI debate, Co. fundamentals and relative valuation.

Executive Summary 
How to play European Software & IT Services  
We consider our sector coverage in the context of five categories:  
1) 
“AI Winners”: our most preferred picks to play AI in the sector (+ recommendations): 
Sage, SAP, OVH 
2) 
“AI Aside” Improving fundamentals at attractive valuation (+ recommendations): 
Capgemini, Amadeus, Nemetschek, SoftwareOne 
3) 
Lacking operational upside, dislocated valuation but “AI burden of proof” today is too 
high to give benefit of the doubt (= recommendations): TeamViewer, Alten, Sopra Steria 
4) 
Operationally positive but valuation limits scope for further upside here (= 
recommendations): Hexagon, Temenos, Ionos 
5) 
AI debate weighs, relative valuation also unattractive and cautious view on 
fundamentals (- recommendations): Dassault Systèmes, Planisware 
SaaS Smash: where are we now? 
In terms of valuation, the sector has bounced off its lows seen during the ‘peak fear’ 
stage earlier this year, but most Software companies and subsegments remain well 
below their 2-yr median valuation multiples. From here we expect Winners to continue to 
re-rate, and Losers to de-rate once again following the recent rally.

We are at an important juncture today; certain pockets of AI Winners have been voted 
(Infrastructure Software inc. Cloud Data Warehouse, and Cybersecurity), and we have 
seen the first ‘cracks’ in AI Losers with downward guidance / financial targets revisions. 
Plenty of Software subsegments sit at the fork in the road, in some cases with the first 
signs of differentiation within the Application layer between those making credible steps 
to the Agentic layer and those who may become marginalised.

Refreshing the BNPP AI Tech Stack: what has happened to each layer? 
We refresh our AI Tech Stack; including refining the position of given companies as 
developments pass.  
Most notable changes may sit in the LLM layer, which has seen both new additions from 
China in Z.Ai, Moonshot AI, Minimax, and the first consolidations with Cohere / Aleph 
Alpha joining forces and Nvidia acquiring Open Model platform Hugging Face. 
We are also starting to see the first signs of Application Software vendors successfully 
moving ‘up the stack’ into the Agentic layer, e.g. Sage, SAP, Salesforce, ServiceNow, 
Microsoft, Workday. Conversely, we are seeing the first disruption impacting financials 
e.g. Adobe, Monday.com, Hubspot, Asana.

Tokenmaxxing to Valuemaxxing: following a period of corporates incentivising AI 
usage / adoption in ‘tokenmaxxing’, we appear to have moved on to ‘valuemaxxing’ (link) 
as 1) the reality of token spend started to hit home and 2) corporates reprioritised output 
over simply adoption.  
Notably, a proxy for token expenditure shown below is down ~50% from May highs, 
driven by a falling price of AI (on a $ per token basis). AI model prices are 
converging towards the same levels at the capability tier, with cheaper models 
(generally open weight or flash versions of frontier models) absorbing more 
workloads at a fraction of the price (link, link). Likewise, the BNPP basket for AI Tech 
Winners is down close to 20% since its highs (BBG: BNPPAITW Index): 
Figure 5: Token Expenditure Index is down ~50% since May highs 
LLM Token Expenditure Index (BBG: “SDLLMTK Index”), Price p/Million tokens 
Source: Bloomberg 
Note that the reality of tokenmaxxing is more balanced in practice. While the costs fell 
per token, the price of LLMs also rose per completed task, as companies move from one-
off chat queries to multi-step agentic workflows that re-read the same context (pressure 
on cache write/ read tokens).

Sector positioning 
In case readers believe the recent sector bounce suggests positioning has normalised, 
fear not. Ownership in European Software / IT Services is at all-time lows, while 
ownership in US Software / IT Services is just below historic median levels: 
Figure 6: Europe and US Software Allocations 
The majority of European funds are now Underweight Software and towards the bottom of the historical range. US main market 
funds are Neutral. 
 
Source:  BNP Paribas Equity Research estimates, Factset 
 
Vertical Software: next on the ‘AI hit list’? 
Summary: Vertical Software has benefitted from a perceived greater moat against AI 
disruption, protecting it from the same degree of valuation multiple compression seen in 
Horizontal Software. While far from being in the clear, ‘peak fear’ may now be behind 
Horizontal Software, with differentiation between emerging Winners/Losers starting to 
materialise. With AI Challengers and Frontier Labs innovating rapidly – particularly in 
Engineering workflow domains – we worry incumbent application vendors in this 
Software subsector may soon have to endure a similar, uncomfortable trajectory to that 
of Horizontal application vendors earlier this year; an indiscriminate risk-off and valuation 
multiple compression before Winners/Losers can be proven out over time.  
We conclude: 1) Vertical software has been relatively more insulated than Horizontal 
software thus far in terms of multiple compression, 2) the AI disruption debate is more 
nascent in Vertical software, risking greater ‘indiscriminate fear’ in the market, 3) 
the signs of differentiation between ‘Winner / Loser’ starting to appear in Horizontal 
software as the burden of proof matures have not yet occurred in Vertical Software, and 
4) the AI challenger narrative is more likely to intensify in the Engineering Software 
sector from here from driven by any / all of: new start-ups including Prometheus / 
PhysicsX / Neural Concept, Frontier AI Labs, and Chinese AI companies (Minimax (+), 
Z.AI (=), Moonshot (provider of Kimi K3). 
In short, we believe the Engineering Software sub-sector could enter a similar period of 
intense scrutiny and indiscriminate fear of AI disruption to that experienced by Horizontal 
Software in Q1 this year, risking weighing on subsector valuation multiples.

DCF Analysis: what is priced in now? 
Summary: in Europe, the most heavily discounted long term operating model risks 
remain in European IT Services, pricing in negative real growth. SAP, Sage and 
Nemetschek all price in roughly half our expected growth. OVH, Temenos, Hexagon, 
Planisware all price in better growth than we expect. 
GenAI disruption fears are prompting the market to recalibrate the long-term viability of 
business models and revisit previously accepted long-term assumptions. 
We take a fresh look at a DCF analysis across our European coverage (based on BNPP 
estimates) to compare the DCF-implied intrinsic value vs. spot price. 
Secondly, we reverse engineer a simulation DCF to force the output to the current share 
prices, flexing growth assumptions only for simplicity (margin assumptions are 
unchanged): 
– In European Software/IT Services, the largest ‘growth discounts’ remain in IT 
Services with 3 of the 4 biggest discrepancies in this subsector: Alten (pricing in -11% 
growth), Sopra Steria (pricing in -7% growth) and Capgemini (pricing in 1% growth).  
– In some cases, the spot price embeds better top line growth than we currently model, 
such as: OVH, Temenos, Hexagon, Planisware, Amadeus.  
– Nemetschek has seen the biggest change since January this year, now priced for 
MSD % growth vs. double-digit % growth at the start of the year.  
– Examples where current Spot prices embed double-digit % revenue CAGR from 
2026-35 include OVHCloud (15%), Planisware (11%) and Hexagon (10%). OVH looks 
particularly dislocated on a DCF basis, because the step-up in our capex estimates from 
FY27 depresses FCF in FY27-28 directly. We therefore value the stock on an 8x FCF 
multiple on steady-state FCF in FY30, rather than a DCF. See our double upgrade of 
OVH (link). 
Figure 12: Implied long-term growth rates in our refreshed reverse-DCF analysis

European Software & IT Services summary  
We summarise our European Software & IT Services coverage recommendations into 
the following five categories: 
1) 
“AI Winners”: our most preferred picks to play AI in the sector (+ recommendations) 
2) 
“AI Aside” Improving fundamentals at attractive valuation (+ recommendations) 
3) 
Lacking operational upside, dislocated valuation but “AI burden of proof” today is too 
high to give benefit of the doubt (= recommendations) 
4) 
Operationally positive but valuation limits scope for further upside here (= 
recommendations) 
5) 
AI debate weighs, relative valuation also unattractive and cautious view on 
fundamentals (- recommendations) 
 
1) 
Most preferred ‘AI Winners’ (all +)  
– Sage: has delivered accelerating organic revenue growth for four consecutive 
quarters into double-digit % territory, driven by a successful AI product rollout and 
commercialisation strategy across its key solutions and customer cohorts. We see scope 
to sustain double-digit organic growth as Sage seeks to leverage the installed base for 
growth - a previously under-utilised resource - reducing reliance on new customer 
acquisition, which we prefer as a lever for growth. See separate report published in 
parallel (link) 
– SAP: armed with arguably the broadest and deepest Enterprise software footprint of 
any vendor globally, together with the #1 position in Enterprise AI fuel – Enterprise Data 
– we see SAP having the greatest potential to benefit from AI over the medium term. 
With that said the journey to success is more complex than Sage given the product and 
customer complexity and requires more ‘proof’ of success than Sage at this juncture. 
See separate report published in parallel (link) 
– OVH: After a ~100% share price rally and 60% EV/EBITDA re-rating YTD, the stock 
prices the Tech Sovereignty narrative (optionality we deliberately excluded) and part of 
the existing-fleet repricing. What it has not priced is GPU fleet expansion. A GPU build 
is unavoidable in our view to meet AI inference demand, and we read that capex as a 
response to excess demand rather than build-and-hope. See separate double-upgrade 
report published in parallel (link) 
2) 
AI Aside, improving fundamentals at attractive valuation (all +)  
– Capgemini: organic growth has recovered to mid-single digit % having suffered both 
cyclical and unfavourable mix headwinds in recent years that we do not believe are 
structural. The WNS acquisition is proving to be a successful strategic move in targeting 
end-to-end process transformation as part of the broader AI-driven digital transformation, 
providing a competitive differentiator. Co. is also repurchasing >10% of its shares (€2bn 
authorisation), addressing its domestic workforce shortcomings in a 2-year restructuring 
programme and has improved the quality of financial reporting KPIs. While IT Services 
as a sector remains out of favour on the structural AI debate as a headcount intensive 
industry, the recipe above trading at 7x P/E or >13% FCF yield (2028) with FCF about to 
return to growth while Co. is priced for negative real growth for the next decade is a low 
enough starting point to make an attractive case to be made here. 
 
 
 


[Page 15]
 
 
BNP Paribas Research 
Software / IT Services 
16 SEPTEMBER 2026 
page 14 
– Amadeus: 2026 has been tough for Amadeus so far, as the Middle East conflict 
weighed on H1 2026 results, prompting the company to adjust down its FY 2026 
guidance when it reported Q2 2026 results. However, IATA’s most recent data points to 
continued air traffic recovery. Combined with continuously strong unitary revenue growth 
and clients onboarding or ramping up in Air IT Solutions and Hospitality, approximately 
9% organic growth is within reach next year. At the same time, investors increasingly 
perceive AI as a lesser threat to Amadeus. Potential catalysts include increased 
buybacks and the announcement of a partnership with a major AI lab for consumer-facing 
travel distribution. Furthermore, at approximately 11x 2027 EV/EBIT versus the 
European software sector median of approximately 13x, valuation remains attractive. 
– Nemetschek: is the fastest growing Software Co. in our sector coverage, driven by 
the mid-20s % growth ‘Build’ division (40% of mix) which we believe can continue for 
several more years as Co. embeds AI functionality into the solution. With operating 
margins in the 30s %, Nemetschek has one of the most attractive financial profiles in the 
global sector. While we here turn incrementally more cautious on the Engineering 
Software sector – of which Nemetschek is a key vendor – we express a relative 
preference Nemetschek (+) over Dassault Systèmes (-). The shares have materially 
underperformed ytd, not catching the sector bounce since mid-July 2026: NEM (37)% 
YTD vs. Software peers having seen some recovery since July now ytd CRM (6)%, NOW 
(13)%, SAP (15)%, WDAY (14)%, TEMN (14)%, SGE (12)%, BSY (19)%, DSY (15)%. 
– SoftwareOne: Co. is selling fast, low‑cost AI‑readiness checks and “AI‑adjacent” 
services (cyber, data) that avoid big transformation budgets, positioning it better than 
generalist IT Services vendors. The company displayed a clear growth inflection over the 
past two quarters, with Crayon now fully consolidated and cost synergies running ahead 
of the initial timeline. Valuation remains compelling vs historical levels and vs peers, at 
10x P/E on FY28 which remains at a 25-30% discount to IT reseller peers. 
3) 
Lacking operational upside. Sector’s ‘AI burden of proof’ too high to give 
benefit of the doubt, even if valuation is dislocated (all =) 
– TeamViewer: the <5x FCF multiple valuation suggests the market believes 
TeamViewer should not exist in <5 years’ time. An extreme valuation dislocation starting 
point. We like the shift into Autonomous Endpoint management and DEX workflows, 
expanding on TeamViewer’s core remote access roots. A ‘mini ServiceNow’ description 
might be a stretch, but the recent strategic collaboration between the pair makes a great 
deal of sense in our view, granting TMV access to key ServiceNow enterprise customers.  
– Holding us back is the business mix: the attractive part of the business is too small 
(~30%) and the part we don’t like is unlikely to become attractive again in our view. 
Valuation is appealing and the Enterprise solution may make TeamViewer a strategic 
acquisition target, but waiting for the organic business mix to turn favourable on its own 
steam as a standalone equity story is not compelling enough for us.  
– Alten: highly headcount-geared operating model, billable engineering hours and 
automation of discrete engineering tasks is less favourable vs. Capgemini where 
Enterprise AI transformation expertise and scale provide upside to the equity case. Co. 
recent AI manifesto does not adequately address risk of AI compressing billable hours 
and pricing power, in our view. We recently downgraded Alten (+ to =) following a >50% 
rally in 3-weeks which we felt had lessened the re-rating scope for now.  
– Sopra Steria: H1 results were better than most feared, but comps are now getting 
tougher for Sopra Steria. The approaching presidential election in France could slow 
momentum in the French public sector, while midterm margin expansion potential 
remains a question mark. Additionally, the company’s AI positioning currently lags behind 
its most advanced peers. 
 


[Page 16]
 
 
BNP Paribas Research 
Software / IT Services 
16 SEPTEMBER 2026 
page 15 
4) 
Plenty to like but valuation limits upside here (all =) 
– Hexagon: has undergone a major strategic and operational turnaround in the last two 
years: new management, new strategy, newly improved financial reporting, a global 
restructuring programme, asset divestitures and an asset carve out (OCTV US), as well 
as a significant inflection in organic growth. This has rewarded shareholders to the tune 
of c.40% since the plan was announced. Hexagon is also a play on a significant long 
term technology wave in Humanoid Robotics; we believe this could bring significant long 
term upside, though near term is a drag on group financials (zero revenue today and 
>€50m opex this year). Given the re-rating to date, and the earnings dilution from 
Robotics today the current ‘all in’ 30x P/E multiple FY27 (inc. Robotics) carries a sizeable 
premium to both Software and Industrials peers alike. We recently downgraded Hexagon 
(+ to =) in a deep dive report here.  
– Temenos: Q2 was disappointing for Temenos. Our latest analysis of underlying SaaS 
growth suggests that the company’s target of >+30% LFL growth in Subscription & SaaS 
for Q3 will be difficult to achieve (BNPP: +21%). Although we believe SaaS could 
accelerate to the mid-teens in FY27 and further into the high-teens in subsequent years, 
we expect Subscription & SaaS to grow only in the mid-single digits (MSD) over the next 
couple of years, compared to the low-double digits (LDD) expected by consensus. This 
is due to our caution regarding underlying market growth in banking software and the 
pace of SaaS adoption. However, the sell-side appears to overestimate cost growth in 
our view, and the company’s AI positioning is ahead of its banking software peers in an 
industry that should itself be relatively less at risk. We rate the stock at Neutral. We 
recently detailed our views on Q3 and midterm growth in an update note (link). 
– Ionos: AI has commoditized basic website‑builder tools but the value of integrated 
“one‑stop‑shop” platforms remains. Attempts to upsell AI features are limited in our view: 
extending base website‑building function faces a ceiling evident at peers, e.g. WIX 
headcount reductions and GoDaddy FY26 guidance cut. 
5) 
Least preferred: falling the wrong side of AI debate, and relative valuation 
screens unattractively against sector alternatives (both -) 
– Dassault Systèmes: as we detail in both this report and a standalone report 
published in parallel, we believe the Engineering Software subsegment could be next to 
face the peak of AI disruption fear while valuation multiples have thus far proved more 
resilient than other pockets of Software leaving room for more multiple compression here. 
DS’ own AI roadmap still has much to be proven in our view. We also see risk of Co. 
resetting its mid-term targets which now embed a sharp growth inflection. Following the 
recent ~15% rally (was +30% through end-Aug) the valuation relative to DS’ top and 
bottom line growth carries a premium to the sector which we feel is unwarranted. We 
downgrade (= to -) in a separate report published in parallel  
– Planisware: after a disappointing 2024/25 where new customer signatures slowed 
prompting a number of profit warnings and negative Consensus estimate revisions, 
Planisware has seen an uptick in operating performance in 2026 helped by Prisma and 
Oscar AI assistants. The shares have rallied >60% since March-2026, now trading on 
c.25x P/E (2027E) against our top and bottom line growth CAGR estimates of 11% the 
implied relative valuation premium has extended. We continue to be more cautious on 
the AI disruption risk to Planning and Project Management (‘PPM’) solutions vs. core 
ERP.

SaaS Smash: where are we now? 
A year into the ‘SaaSpocalypse’: how AI rebalanced power among the AI tech stack 
The AI vs. Software battle is now just over a year in the making, having begun in earnest 
when OpenAI released GPT-5 in August 2025.  
We outline the ‘AI Fear Index’ experienced at the Software sector level, and how we see 
that trajectory ahead. 
We are at an important juncture today; certain pockets of AI Winners have been voted 
(Infrastructure Software inc. Cloud Data Warehouse, and Cybersecurity), and we have 
seen the first ‘cracks’ in AI Losers with downward guidance / financial targets revisions. 
Plenty of Software subsegments sit at the fork in the road, in some cases with the first 
signs of differentiation within the Application layer between those making credible steps 
to the Agentic layer and those who may become marginalised. 
In terms of valuation; the sector has bounced off its lows seen during the ‘peak fear’ 
stage earlier this year, but most Software companies and subsegments remain well 
below their 2-yr median valuation multiples. From here we expect Winners to continue to 
re-rate, and Losers to de-rate once again following the recent rally.

Refreshing the BNPP AI Tech Stack: what has happened to each layer? 
We refresh our AI Tech Stack; including refining the position of given companies as 
developments pass.  
Most notable changes may sit in the LLM layer, which has seen both new additions from 
China in Z.Ai, Moonshot AI, Minimax, and the first consolidations with Cohere / Aleph 
Alpha joining forces and Nvidia acquiring Open Model platform Hugging Face. 
Figure 14: BNPP AI Tech Stack 
Refreshed with 1) updated commentary at each layer of the stack, 2) refined Co. categorisation 
Source:  BNP Paribas Equity Research estimates 
A closer look by layer: 
Agentic tools layer  
Incumbent Applications: first signs of credibility in transforming to the Agentic layer are 
being seen among select incumbent Application vendors. This is manifesting in 1) AI 
products gaining traction with customers / ‘adoption’, and/or 2) Monetisation reaching 
critical mass in terms of a tipping point of probability of success, and/or 3) AI driving 
Revenue growth reacceleration.  
Examples include: Microsoft Copilot reaching 30m paid seats, ServiceNow AI 
surpassing $1bn ACV with multiple positive AI SKU adoption datapoints, Salesforce 
Agentforce ARR surpassing $1.5bn ARR and guiding for Revenue growth reacceleration, 
SAP cloud backlog growth reaccelerating and Data Cloud momentum scaling, Sage 
organic total revenue growth accelerating for fourth successive quarter, Workday AI 
ARR approaching $600m (+200% y.y. growth).

page 18 
Challenger Natives: hype among the horizontal AI natives (in CRM / HCM / ERP 
domains) has cooled slightly, though AI challengers continue to 1) scale through $100m+ 
revenue run rates, and 2) successfully raise funding ‘up rounds’ or in some cases 3) 
become acquired by incumbents (e.g. SAP acquiring PriorLabs, Dremio, Reltio). We 
believe these horizontal challengers will soon approach a critical ‘proof point’ frontier 
where they need to be scaling sufficiently with evidence of disrupting pockets of the 
market before the incumbents build enough of a moat with multi-billion dollar AI revenue 
streams and adoption reaches critical mass (e.g. Salesforce/Workday AI SKUs still ‘only’ 
comprise ~6% Total Revenue today). 
On the other hand, some Incumbent Application hopefuls have started to fade; Adobe 
and Intuit both with credible Agentic AI solutions yet facing challenges and providing 
disappointments to the market despite their AI product offerings. 
We see growing interest and progress in vertical AI challengers, with the likes of 
PhysicsX ($2.4bn valuation) and Prometheus ($41bn valuation) in Engineering Software 
gaining attention as potentially disruptive start-ups, while Schneider’s $3.1bn acquisition 
of Industrial Data Cloud provider Cognite was notable.  
LLM layer 
Frontier AI labs continue to innovate but the incremental ‘fear index’ ripple effect 
on related equity markets is diminishing most notably since Summer compared to Q1 
this year. Recent examples of model releases with less incremental shockwave impact 
include: GPT-6 Astra (Sep-2026), Claude Fable (Jun-2026), Gemini Flash (Jul-2026). 
Frontier AI competition intensifying: Mistral acquired Emmi AI (May) doubling down 
on Engineering AI moving ‘up the stack’, as well as increasing competition from China 
open-weight models such as Moonshot (Kimi K3), Minimax (+) and Z.AI (=). Additionally, 
Meta released its first ‘personal’ AI agent (Consumer) ‘Muse Spark’.  
First signs of blurring of lines between tech stack layers, and open vs. closed 
model debate intensifies: Nvidia acquired Hugging Face (link) Sep-2026 for $13bn to 
boost open model platform capability. This follows Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s July-
2026 Open Letter championing open-weight models, in the aftermath of the US 
administration banning access to Claude Fable-5 / Mythos-5 models in June-2026. 
Further consolidation, this time at the model layer: Canadian AI lab Cohere agreed 
to acquire (/ merge in 90/10 ownership of combined entity) with German AI lab Aleph 
Alpha (Apr-2026), combined valuation of ~$20bn.   
Tokenmaxxing to Valuemaxxing: following a period of corporates incentivising AI 
usage / adoption in ‘tokenmaxxing’, we appear to have moved on to ‘valuemaxxing’ (link) 
as 1) the reality of token spend started to hit home and 2) corporates reprioritised output 
over simply adoption.  
Notably, a proxy for token expenditure shown below is down ~50% from May highs. 
Likewise, the BNPP basket for AI Tech Winners is down close to 20% since its highs 
(BBG: BNPPAITW Index):

Returns on AI Capex to shift into view as IPOs loom: with leading frontier labs likely 
to tap public equity markets soon, there is growing attention around return on AI 
investment of these major players.  
Cloud data warehouse / AI Infrastructure Software winner status confirmed 
Our confidence in the Cloud data warehouse layer has been reinforced, with strong (and 
accelerating) momentum seen in this layer of the stack, with Snowflake seeing its Product 
revenue growth accelerate to 37% (shares +50% ytd), Datadog seeing +30% group 
revenue growth in FY26 (shares +66% ytd). The logic holds that AI is most value 
accretive when it can leverage a harmonised data estate beneath it, and the Cloud Data 
Warehouse is the enabler.  
Beyond data infrastructure, absolute compute (IaaS) has also benefitted as enterprises 
buy into a server price upcycle that has repriced every layer of the compute chain. 
Neoclouds are the most direct beneficiaries (Nebius (=) / CoreWeave (+) guiding for 
FY26 revenue growth of 150%/ 540% respectively) while hyperscalers benefit from 
frontier labs capex driving Microsoft Azure growth to 43% ccy, AWS growth to 37% and 
Google Cloud Platform growth to 82%. 
Application layer: signs of Winner / Loser status starting to be proven out  
The market did not seek to differentiate between Winners / Losers in the application layer 
in Q1, with most vendors and sub-segments de-rating sharply. May-2026 saw the first 
subsector reversal in Cybersecurity with leaders’ share prices doubling ytd. 
Progression through Q2 into Q3 saw two important and related changes: 1) the impact  
of AI is starting to manifest in the financials of perceived Winners / Losers: positive 
Q2 results from the likes of ServiceNow, Atlassian, SAP, Salesforce, Sage showed AI 
product momentum gaining pace becoming visible in financials, while companies such 
as Adobe, Hubspot, Monday.com, Asana delivered disappointing results, and 2) the 
market starting to differentiate in terms of share price reaction and valuation, a 
notable change from previous earnings seasons when even positive results were greeted 
with negative share price / valuation reactions.

We see a blend of strategies (highlighted above) among application vendors morphing 
up the stack into the agentic layer: some remain LLM agnostic (SAP, ServiceNow) while 
others opt to form more direct partnerships (Salesforce with Anthropic) or in some cases 
are building their own proprietary models (Sage on AWS Bedrock and SAP RPT-1.5 
relational data model). Whichever route is taken, select incumbent application vendors 
are starting to show commercial traction, which is becoming visible in their financials.  
Below we illustrate Application Software subsegment progress through the ‘AI Fear 
Index’ trajectory.  
We believe the market has voted Infrastructure and Cybersecurity Software as proven 
‘AI Winners’ this year. ERP is starting to emerge as a category AI Winner helped by SAP, 
Sage and Oracle, while CRM / HCM categories are coming out of ‘peak fear’ reaching 
the fork in the road between Winner / Loser paths. 
We believe Vertical Software has yet to enter the ‘peak fear’ phase experienced by 
Horizontal Software in Q1 this year. And with AI Challenger / LLM innovation ramping up 
we see risk of entering a painful transition phase ahead. Other Verticals such as 
Financials (‘Fins’) across Banking / Payment Services are still in the ‘Benefit of Doubt’ 
phase in our view.

The AI debate is more nascent in Vertical Software (Banking, Engineering, Travel, etc), 
having suffered less outright market fear in Q1 but equally has yet to manifest in the way 
the Horizontal Software application has started to. The moat argument remains – 
complex IP workflows, regulated end-markets, AI bringing more complexity and reliance 
on these solutions – although the AI challenger narrative is intensifying from both Frontier 
Labs and AI Native start ups. 
IT Services: in the ‘burden of proof’ camp with sector trends yet to inflect 
Demand trends remain largely unchanged at the sector level, with no meaningful or 
broad-based recovery seen thus far. Organic growth trajectories at many of the large 
GSIs (Accenture (=), Infosys (+), TCS (+)) has remained mixed with no clear sight of 
growth recovery and generally underwhelming FY guidance, while Accenture's FQ3’26 
bookings were disappointing. Capgemini has seen organic growth accelerate from 0.3% 
in FY25 to 5.1% in Q2’26, though Co. commentary indicates this is not due to a 
meaningful change in operating environment trends rather lapping easier comparators 
and Continental Europe becoming less of a headwind.  
European focused smaller players have fared better: Alten re-rated c.40% on 12m 
forward P/E after a strong Q2 and guidance raise, while Sopra Steria also raised FY26 
guidance in Q2. Though recent political newsflow has been digested negatively with 
French Finance Minister Eric Lombard calling for AI productivity gains at IT Services 
vendors to be shared with government contracts and Mr David Miel calling outright for 
their contract pricing to be lowered.  
The +23% sector re-rating since end-June leaves IT Services at 10x, still 30% below 2-
yr median. Investor sentiment remains cautious on headcount-intensive operating model, 
skills obsolescence and pricing-driven margin compression.

Valuation bounce and revisions inflection: now what? 
Summary: Software valuations have bounced off their lows, helped by a more positive 
Q2 results season with a higher mix of ‘beat and raise’ quarters after a period of upgrade 
absence, together with isolated examples of either 1) AI commercialisation datapoints 
helping substantiate claims of successfully repositioning up into the Agentic layer, and/or 
2) top line growth KPIs reaccelerating. Despite the re-rating, the sector remains well 
below historic valuation levels, reflecting a general market reluctance to return to the 
sector.  
European Software de-rated by 28% between Jan-Jun 2026 and IT Services by 21%. 
European Software now trades on 18x P/FCF, at parity with the US 10yr risk free yield 
(vs 30% premium start-2026). Despite the re-rating in recent weeks, the sector remains 
well below historical multiples.  
Figure 19: European Software now 14% above the lows, but still discounted  
P/ FCF multiple 12m fwd (Bloomberg Consensus) vs. Spot US 10yr yield (inverse) 
Source: BNP Paribas Equity Research estimates, Bloomberg as of 11 September 2026. Stocks include: SAP, 
Dassault Systemes, Nemetschek, Sage, Hexagon, Temenos, Amadeus, Planisware, TeamViewer.  
Subsector valuation de-rating / re-rating so far 
Within Global Software, Horizontal SaaS de-rating was more acute (54%) between 
January and June 2026 while Vertical software maintained its premium – Engineering 
Software only de-rated by 17% over the same period for example. Cybersecurity re-rated 
sharply by 50%.

Vertical software: next on the ‘AI Hist List? 
Summary: Vertical Software has benefitted from a perceived greater moat against AI 
disruption, protecting it from the same degree of valuation multiple compression seen in 
Horizontal Software. While far from being in the clear, ‘peak fear’ may now be behind 
Horizontal Software, with differentiation between emerging Winners/Losers starting to 
materialise. With AI Challengers and Frontier Labs innovating rapidly – particularly in 
Engineering workflow domains – we worry incumbent application vendors in this 
Software subsector may soon have to endure a similar, uncomfortable trajectory to that 
of Horizontal application vendors earlier this year; an indiscriminate risk-off and valuation 
multiple compression before Winners/Losers can be proven out over time.  
We conclude: 1) Vertical software has been relatively more insulated than Horizontal 
software thus far in terms of multiple compression, 2) the AI disruption debate is more 
nascent in Vertical software, risking greater ‘indiscriminate fear’ in the market, 3) 
the signs of differentiation between ‘Winner / Loser’ starting to appear in Horizontal 
software as the burden of proof matures have not yet occurred in Vertical Software, and 
4) the AI challenger narrative is more likely to intensify in the Engineering Software 
sector from here from driven by any / all of: new start-ups including Prometheus / 
PhysicsX / Neural Concept, Frontier AI Labs and Chinese AI companies (Minimax (+), 
Z.AI (=), Moonshot (provider of Kimi K3).  
In short, we believe the Engineering Software sub-sector could enter a similar period of 
intense scrutiny and indiscriminate fear of AI disruption to that experienced by Horizontal 
Software in Q1 this year, risking weighing on subsector valuation multiples.

Vertical software has been more insulated than Horizontal 
software so far from the AI fuelled “SaaSpocalypse”  
The AI story so far: Vertical software more insulated vs. Horizontal 
A year ago, the commonly held view (including by us link) was that Horizontal SaaS was 
more vulnerable to AI disruption while Vertical SaaS was more insulated owing to more 
IP, more complexity and more niche domain knowledge embedded in industry-specific 
workflows. YTD price action through June had been consistent with that view. 
Horizontal app stocks have de-rated by 28% YTD on a 12m fwd. P/E basis, widening 
their discount to 2-yr median to 30%. In comparison, Engineering Software stocks ‘only’ 
de-rated by 10% YTD, while Cybersecurity re-rated by 65% on strong earnings execution 
and growing investor conviction that agentic AI deployments will require accelerated 
security infrastructure investments.

Pinpointing where AI could disrupt Engineering Software  
Summary: the Engineering Software workflow has several important stages. We believe 
the ‘Start’ (geometry kernels) and ‘Finish’ (verification into production) stages may be 
insulated, but the ‘Middle’ (concept design, geometry clean up, meshing, simulation) risks 
attracting intense competition from 1) AI Challenger start-ups like PhysicsX / Prometheus 
and 2) Frontier AI Labs and Chinese AI companies (Minimax (+), Z.AI (=), Moonshot 
(provider of Kimi K3). Incumbent vendors do have ways to fight back: owning the 
geometry kernels is a key moat, and the end-to-end platform ‘suite’ is another. We also 
highlight how the segment’s economic model is likely to change towards tokenisation, 
which brings both risk and opportunity.  
Unpacking the Engineering workflow 
A typical workflow starts when an engineer creates a component in a CAD (Computer 
Aided Design) software. To evaluate its strength, the geometric object is exported to a 
simulation tool (which often strips away some fine‑level geometric information).  
The user runs the analysis delivering iterative reports. After the results are approved, a 
manufacturing engineer imports the geometry into a CAM (Computer Aided 
Manufacturing) system to generate toolpaths, once again converting file formats and 
risking the loss of tolerance or material specifications as the workflow can shift software 
programme between stages.  
In parallel, there is a PLM (Product Lifecycle Management) software check-in running 
across the whole chain. A PLM software locks in design progress, updates the central 
system with the latest files, including preventing overwriting different stages of work.  
At any step of the workflow, if a part fails, the entire chain runs in reverse through each 
handoff.  
Figure 33: Overview of Engineering Software and competitive landscape  
Source:  BNP Paribas Equity Research estimates, YouTube  
 


[Page 34]
 
 
BNP Paribas Research 
Software / IT Services 
16 SEPTEMBER 2026 
page 33 
Figure 34: A multi-step journey creates friction as requires different systems and human inputs 
Illustrative journey in Engineering Software  
Source:  BNP Paribas Equity Research estimates 
Four characteristics of this journey are conventionally cited as the Engineering Software 
moat:  
– Fragmentation of technology stack: The journey is fragmented between multiple 
stages which can span numerous software vendors and solutions, each executing a 
specific task or part of the workflow. Changing any single stage requires careful re-
tooling, and often human capital re-training.  
– Specialist gating: Geometry clean-up, meshing and solver configuration each 
require a specialist engineer. The tools have their own language which is learned by 
engineers at university, with extremely high opportunity switching costs to re-train (DS 
CATIA vs. Siemens NX in 3D CAD, for example).   
– Regulatory validation. In regulated end markets like Aerospace, only specific 
validated solvers are accepted (e.g. Star-CCM+ by Siemens or Ansys Fluent). While 
regulation is not a permanent barrier, validation for a new entrant / solver is slow and 
costly.  
– Scarce data: Physics training data is proprietary and fragmented between 
incumbents. Data is costly to generate, up to US$1,000+ per simulation run for modelling 
fluids (‘CFD’ Computational Fluid Dynamics) for example. When data is not available one 
has to generate data robust enough such that the model can be trained adequately. 
Hence the cost is often down to compute / clusters of CPUs. The ground truth for models 
powering Engineering Software is laws of physics, where there is no room for 
approximation (versus LLMs which infer based on text semantics), and hallucination 
carries human and legal consequences. 
– Geometric kernel IP: Every 3D design tool sits on a geometric kernel. This is the 
engine that turns shapes into precise, manufacturable solids. Geometric kernels are 
licensed by a limited number of vendors (Siemens, Dassault Systems, PTC….). This is 
not easily addressable by AI. Geometry has to be exact so any new CAD model 
generating a design generally still needs a kernel to make it real.  
 


[Page 35]
 
 
BNP Paribas Research 
Software / IT Services 
16 SEPTEMBER 2026 
page 34 
Construction Software  
AEC software is deemed to be harder to disrupt because the moat is a function of end 
market structure rather than technical. The buyer is not a single enterprise but a 
consortium of counterparties ( owner, architect, engineers, main contractors, 
subcontractors, insurers , regulators, …)  so the platform's value lies in being the single 
shared record.  
This record sits on top of outputs that are legally binding: a BIM model (“Building 
Information Modeling”) mandates all counterparties to create and share structured, data-
rich models to ensure compliance. There is first mover advantage here, as incumbents 
that arrived first have accumulated years of configuration depth in a sector with among 
the lowest levels of digitalization.  
Figure 35: Construction Software moat is a mix of regulation, data system-of-record, tailored features 
3D model switch using GraphiSoft (Nemetschek)  
 
Legally-binding BIM collaboration using Autodesk  
 
 
Source:  Nemetshcek, Autodesk  
Where AI could potentially become a threat 
Summary: the middle stages of the Engineering Software workflow face most risk of AI 
disruption in our view; conceptual design, simulation, neural surrogate models, clean up 
meshing in particular. The threat lies in both AI native challengers (PhysicsX, 
Prometheus, Neural Concept), Frontier AI Labs and Chinese AI companies (Minimax (+), 
Z.AI (=), Moonshot (provider of Kimi K3). If customers can cheaply and quickly outsource 
these parts of the workflow to new competition, the royalty rent from owning the 
start/finish parts of the workflow may reduce over time. This will also likely accelerate the 
need to transition to Cloud, bringing operational and financial near term risks, while an 
economic model transition towards tokenisation and consumption is also likely to be 
necessary.  
We believe AI risk is highest in potentially compressing the middle parts of the workflow 
(2: Design generation and 3: Meshing / solvers) and lower at the start/finish phases of 
the workflow (Start = “geometric kernel”, or a core mathematical engine that builds, 
calculates, and validates 2D and 3D shapes inside a CAD software / Finish = final stage 
most often is human validation).  
 


[Page 36]
 
 
BNP Paribas Research 
Software / IT Services 
16 SEPTEMBER 2026 
page 35 
Figure 36: BNPP AI Risk analysis of Engineering Workflow stages 
Source:  BNP Paribas Equity Research estimates 
Diving into the middle phases of the Engineering Software workflow 
In Engineering Software, to ensure an object can perform as intended in the real world, 
traditionally a physics simulation is run using a solver. Solvers were historically based on 
numerical models. Each simulation required equations run iteratively on each cell of the 
mesh to obtain a prediction.  
Consequently, the solver used iterative inputs from the previous steps (meshing and 
geometry clean-up): this process was both time consuming and required specialist 
engineers. Each simulation required a ‘new run’ based on changed mesh / geometry 
variables.  
AI fundamentally changes how underlying models perform simulation. A new type 
of model – neural surrogate models – are pre-trained on the relationship between a set 
of parameters and their outcomes. A neural surrogate model skips this iterative loop: it 
has already learned the mapping from input conditions to outcomes, so it can infer the 
result in a single forward pass and return a prediction instantly without first having to 
revisit the original ‘teacher’ (geometric kernel).  
Figure 37: From numerical to neural surrogate models  
Source: Emmi AI 
 
 
 


[Page 37]
 
 
BNP Paribas Research 
Software / IT Services 
16 SEPTEMBER 2026 
page 36 
AI challengers are now able to train their own physics model for simulation but 
also for geometry generation.  
AI Challengers can bypass the data bottleneck by either partnering with incumbents 
(PhysicsX AI has access to Siemens solver data following a partnership in 2024, which 
we understand is focused on automotive, aerospace and manufacturing applications 
based on recent management interactions) or by jointly developing models with 
customers directly on their data (Luminary AI/ Honda): 
PhysicsX AI 
UK-based AI company founded in 2019 by Jacomo Corbo (CEO) and Robin Tuluie, both 
ex-F1 engineers. Co. was valued at $2.4bn in June 2026, with backers including 
Siemens, Nvidia among others. PhysicsX developed its own underlying models for 
both geometry (CAD) and simulation (physics solver) and is building an 
engineering platform on top where customers can access third party models and run 
the journey from design to simulation. The platform is mostly deployed across customers 
in automotive, aerospace, and industrial machinery. PhysicsX AI has been partnering 
with Siemens since 2024 for go-to market collaboration, solver data access.  
Emmi AI – acquired by MistralAI  
Founded in 2024 in Austria by Johannes Brandstetter, Dennis Just and Miks Mikelsons 
as a spinout of JKU Linz (Linz University) and NXAI (Austrian AI lab focused on building 
a European LLM). Mistral acquired Emmi AI in May 2026 with 30+ researchers joining 
Mistral's Science and Applied AI teams. Emmi AI developed its own foundation 
models trained on physical laws, replacing CFD/DEM solvers outright (AB-UPT model), 
aimed at aerospace, automotive, semis and energy. NeuralDEM, its end-to-end deep 
learning alternative to CFD - DEM Multiphysics, is now open source. Shortly after the 
acquisition, MistralAI signed deals with Airbus to integrate advanced AI across its military 
and defence operations, and BMW to develop industry-specific AI models for crash 
simulation workflows. 
Neural Concept 
Neural Concept is an EPFL spinout founded in 2019 by Pierre Baqué (CEO), Théophile 
Allard (both ex-EPFL PhDs), Thomas von Tschammer and others. Co. raised $136m 
total to-date (undisclosed valuation). They tackle the platform layer, more particularly 
CAD (geometry) rather than the solver: Instant generation of CAD-ready geometry from 
high-level design ideas, Exploration of 10 to 1,000 times more design variants per 
iteration, Explore trade-offs across performance objectives, rank and filter design spaces, 
and deploy at enterprise scale using Neural Concept while the CAD formats generated 
are compatible with incumbents (Siemens NX, SolidWorks, Catia, OnShape, 
SpaceClaim). Co. serves automotive, aerospace & defence, energy, semis and 
consumer electronics, with partnerships with NVIDIA, Siemens, Ansys, Microsoft and 
AWS.  
Luminary  
Luminary was founded in San Mateo in 2019 by Jason Lango (ex-Bracket Computing) 
and Juan Alonso (founder of Stanford's Aerospace Design Lab, ex-NASA); Pete 
Schlampp is now CEO.  It was incubated by Sutter Hill Ventures, raised a $72m Series 
B in September 2025 led by N47 (ex-Next47, Siemens-affiliated) with Sutter Hill and 
NVIDIA's NVentures, on an earlier $115 -187m previously raised financing (valuation 
undisclosed). They target the model layer. Luminary’s differentiator is pre-trained 
SHIFT foundation models built with named OEM partners - SHIFT-SUV with Honda 
for automotive aero, SHIFT-Wing with Otto Aerospace, released open-source with 
NVIDIA. That partner-generated training data is the clearest answer to the sector's core 
constraint: data. 
 
 


[Page 38]
 
 
BNP Paribas Research 
Software / IT Services 
16 SEPTEMBER 2026 
page 37 
Monolith – Acquired by CoreWeave  
Founded in 2016 in London by Dr Richard Ahlfeld. The company was born out of his 
research work at Imperial. Raised only £8.5m Series A in 2021 out of ~£10.6m in total. 
CoreWeave acquired Monolith in October 2025 (terms undisclosed). Positioning was 
narrower: simulation and test-driven machine learning capabilities, test plan optimisation 
and calibration across battery, automotive, aerospace, defence, industrial and medical. 
The pitch is: fewer expensive physical test rigs, i.e. less need for custom-built 
testing setups that engineers use to recreate real-world forces and prove a design 
works safely before production). For CoreWeave we believe it was a verticalization 
play to grow footprint in a sticky end market. 
Potential impact on incumbents: reducing steps in the workflow journey with less 
reliance on specialist meshing and solver engineering. Potential threat to 
incumbents’ seat-based revenue model. 
In theory, nothing prevents incumbents from evolving their numerical solver models to 
neural surrogate models. Compute environment is generally free (PhysicsNeMo) and 
incumbents are already partnering with Nvidia.  
How incumbents face a risk of cannibalisation 
Collapsing the multi-step workflow is technically achievable, but commercially shrinks the 
volume of seats / licenses sold over the numerous middle steps for incumbent vendors. 
Furthermore, this could have implications for the commercial model of incumbent 
software vendors as ‘value transfer risk’ materialises. 
Comparable outputs can be generated 10,000x faster, reducing simulation 
compute requirements. Once trained on a deterministic model built by an incumbent 
vendor a probabilistic model can simulate a system without recourse to their ‘teacher’.  
This represents a challenge to incumbent Engineering Software vendors providing 
traditional solvers and fundamental core deterministic physics models: a single 
simulation run may no longer require any use of its HPC/ CFD intensive modules.  
The use of these models per design iteration may be vastly reduced. With the customer 
may needing less of them and so require fewer ‘seats’, with value transfer occurring to 
new challengers with smart statistical surrogate capabilities. 
Figure 38: Neural Concept achieved ~0.7% mean relative error and cut CFD requirements by 97.5% 
Source:  Neural Concept, BNP Paribas Equity Research estimates 
 


[Page 39]
 
 
BNP Paribas Research 
Software / IT Services 
16 SEPTEMBER 2026 
page 38 
Frontier lab advances into geometric designs and production, as well as AEC 
workflows. This risk disrupting mass market CAD / Manufacturing / AEC 
incumbent solutions 
Frontier Labs are expanding into CAD-style software. In June 2026, Claude Fable 5 was 
reported to have built and refined proportions of a 3D-printable lighthouse based on a 
single text prompt.  
More recently, Moonshot’s Kimi K3 (China based open-weight AI model) was reportedly 
able to automate the MEP modelling (Mechanical Electrical and Plumbing) of a 190-room 
hotel, a task normally requiring specialized BIM software from the likes of Nemetschek / 
Autodesk (plus a team of human architects / engineers). 
Figure 39: Frontier LLMs can generate designs historically entrenched in complex software workflows 
Kimi K3 automated 3D modelling of a building  
 
Fable 5 generated a VibeCAD editor itself  
 
Source:  Substack, Anthropic 
Most recently in September-2026 OpenAI released GPT-6 Astra, for which its 
commercial launch video demonstrates firstly the creation of an animation sketch of a 
rocket before transforming it into more detailed 3D model in Blender - an open source 
computer generated graphics tool – before finally sending the designed image to ‘build’ 
phase using a 3D printer (below).  
While this is still a long way from high end 3D CAD / Manufacturing workflows of Dassault 
Systèmes’ CATIA / Delmia or Siemens’ NX / TeamCenter, we are now seeing the first 
examples of Frontier Labs evolving into geometry ‘production grade’ design and 
manufacturing which could start to disrupt the mass market solutions such as Solidworks 
(DSY) / SolidEdge (SIE) / Creo (PTC).  
Figure 40: OpenAI GPT-6 Astra can now extend to geometry spec and production grade workflows 
GPT-6 Astra launched Sep-2026. Screenshots are from OpenAI launch video  
GPT-6 Astra can now create Geometry-based CAD files 
 …and extend to production / manufacturing (e.g. 3D printing)

BNP Paribas Research

16 SEPTEMBER 2026 
page 39 
Incumbents’ commercial model likely to evolve 
We see a growing need to evolve the economic model for Engineering Software vendors 
away from human engineer ‘seats’ to a tokenised consumption model. Some horizontal 
application vendors are further along this journey than Engineering Software vendors in 
developing and commercialising this shift, notably Salesforce / Workday / SAP. 
Most incumbent large Engineering Software vendors (Dassault Systemes, Siemens, 
Autodesk, Bentley, Nemetschek, PTC...) sell solutions on a p/seat basis, with some 
Enterprise License Agreements (ELA) in place for larger customers.  
If the Revenue model evolves to ‘charge for usage,’ what might that look like? We see 
two possible future ways to monetise customer usage of, or the value created by, 
Engineering Software products. Vendors could move to either: 
– Usage based billing. Either by charging for time using elements of their software IP 
or charging for compute. A move to tokenise the Engineering Software being offered is 
likely a precursor to attempting to monetise the product, without recourse to charging for 
individual licenses/ seats. 
– Outcome pricing. Charging customers per design/ or design tweak. Charging per 
simulation, per mesh, for clean-up etc… Once again tokenisation would appear to be a 
necessary step on the road to this revenue model. Measurement of the outcome may not 
always be easy – the customer may not always feel like value was created, especially 
when costly iterations lead to design ‘dead ends.’ 
In both cases, it seems to us, that tokenisation is a necessary step on the path to 
measuring – and therefore being able to charge – customers in proportion to the amount 
they consume, or the value they gain from incumbent engineering software vendors. 
Some early examples of this include: Dassault Systèmes “Blue Tokens” (launched 
Jul-2026, details below) and Ansys “Elastic Units” which are  pay-per-use . SimScale 
uses a usage-based pricing model based on "core hours," which represent the 
computational time required for simulations. Users pay for the number of core hours 
consumed during simulation runs. 
Dassault Systèmes Blue Tokens overview: DS announced its Virtual Companions at 
the start of 2026. Recently (Jul-2026) it has revealed the first commercial details for these 
companions. DS describes the companions as; ‘providing units of work, using structured 
industry knowledge to interpret user intent, reason in an industrial context, take decisions 
and generate outcomes grounded in science and industry-accurate reality’.  
– Domains: Blue Tokens are the commercialisation of DS’ AI Agents / Copilots “AURA” 
(Programme Management), “LEO” (Engineering) and “MARIE” (Deep Science) 
– Pricing: $1,250 p/25,000 tokens, or $0.05 p/token unit price 
– Environment: must be hosted on Public Cloud only 
– Duration: Blue Tokens expire after 12 months if unused 
– Permissions: Blue Tokens are shared at the tenant level (not user level) 
We understand DS will transition towards three types of credits / tokens;  
– Knowledge: licensing / royalties for standards or training data. We believe this could 
point to monetisation of its underlying Kernels discussed above 
– Know-How: extracting and indexing process and design data  
– Work: computational cost. Likely to be the purest ‘headcount seat-based offset’ 
 


[Page 41]
 
 
BNP Paribas Research 
Software / IT Services 
16 SEPTEMBER 2026 
page 40

Start / End phases of workflow at less risk, for now 
Validation / accountability 
While Frontier Labs and AI challengers can now produce assemblies that look right or 
function to a low-end production grade standard, none have yet produced something 
buildable to high grade engineering accuracy. And at the back end, a prediction you 
cannot interrogate in every last detail cannot be certified in these highly technical and 
usually regulated markets: to enter production, you need visibility into the underlying 
equations. Cheaper and faster is not sufficient; accountability requires a validated solver. 
We used the MARB benchmark - an open-source benchmark that provides a standard 
scenario and prompt to build an object across different LLMs, testing the asymmetry of 
the output, the discrepancy vs the intended shape, etc. Fable ranks as Level 1 alongside 
other LLMs, while professional CAD software ranks as Level 4-7. Although Fable ranked 
better than most LLMs, it is not an engineer-grade model. 
Figure 43: MARB benchmark levels  
Source: MARB  
Figure 44: Fable 5 ranks as the best LLMs for CAD tasks with the lowest amount 
of billed tokens and lower median GAP discrepancy, but still far behind 
traditional CAD software   
Source: MARB 
 


[Page 44]
 
 
BNP Paribas Research 
Software / IT Services 
16 SEPTEMBER 2026 
page 43 
The ‘kernel’ computation layer may be immune, but could see less traffic 
The geometric kernel is the layer beneath any CAD model (geometry). Every CAD model 
depends on a geometric kernel or ‘math engine’ which solves a design operation into a 
valid solid, and outputs an exact mathematical description of the resulting shape. Kernels 
produce a reference file called a boundary representation  (“B-rep”), with an explicit 
record of what connects to what.  
Figure 45: A geometric kernel produces a B-rep, or boundary representation – a 
shape made of “NURBS” surfaces (non-uniform rational B-spline) stitched 
together by control points  
Source: Demystifyingplm.com  
Ownership of geometric kernels is concentrated. Siemens and Dassault Systemes are 
the major providers of geometric kernel today, licensing their proprietary kernel to other 
CAD vendors, including to each other – for example Solidworks uses Parasolid, the 
kernel of Siemens. (Solidworks was a standalone business acquired by DS in 1997 for 
$310m and now generates >€1bn in annual revenues). 
Figure 46: Which geometric kernels are used by major CAD software providers?

AI challengers are not building competing kernels, although this is technically feasible 
(GitHub tutorial). They are building products that reduce how often the reference file is 
required. A neural surrogate model can infer geometry without invoking a full kernel, and 
a clean NURBS representation becomes less central in such workflows. 
 


[Page 45]
 
 
BNP Paribas Research 
Software / IT Services 
16 SEPTEMBER 2026 
page 44 
Nvidia is also giving away a free tool which limits the reliance on CPU-bound geometric 
kernels. Warp is a free, open-source Python library. Rather than offloading geometry and 
spatial queries to a traditional external CAD or geometric kernel (like Parasolid, ACIS, or 
Open CASCADE), Warp lets developers write GPU kernels and runs them on the GPU 
for higher performance.  
Incumbents owning a geometric kernel derive a marginal rent for its licensing, as 
workflows are intertwined with the reference shape produced by kernels. The risk is a 
decline in how frequently the workflow passes through this reference file. The rent per 
interaction is unchanged; the number of interactions falls. Incumbents owning the 
Kernel could raise prices in order to offset any decline in interaction volume, e.g. 
Knowledge Units from DS mentioned above. 
How incumbents can fight back  
Owning the end-to-end platform suite a genuine moat, as is hardware 
integration 
There is an evident disruption of the model layer, for both geometry and physics 
simulation. Startups are developing their own models (PhysicsX AI, Emmi AI, 
Luminary…). But we also think there is a risk of model commoditisation, due to:  
– Architectures get published: New models’ underlying architectures are soon 
published in papers and open‑source repositories, so any early edge disappears quickly 
(Emmi AI AB-UPT, Luminary SHIFT-WING…).   
– The GPU tooling is third-party: Nvidia's PhysicsNeMo environment, or an 
equivalent, is a stated input to building a surrogate. Innovation is hardware-adjacent, and 
nothing prevents incumbents from using Nvidia compute environment to convert their 
models into neural surrogate models themselves.  
– Data partnerships dilute the data moat: Incumbents can partner with startups, such 
as Siemens with PhysicsX AI. Incumbents gain access to disruptive technologies that 
eventually increase the use of their platforms despite a risk of revenue cannibalization, 
while startups gain data knowledge and go-to market capabilities.  
If the model commoditises, defensibility moves to a less glamorous layer: the 
platform. The largest incumbents own the engineer's workflow end-to-end, integrating 
CAD design, Simulation, Manufacturing and PLM layers, even carrying the customer 
through regulatory qualification. We see this as a key moat for incumbent vendors, 
particularly where the platform footprint is sizeable (see our market ‘wheel’ below): 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


[Page 46]
 
 
BNP Paribas Research

We note: AI challengers like PhysicsX AI are now building an interoperable platform on 
top of its proprietary models, to grant access to its customers to third-party models.  
Figure 48: PhysicsX AI platform coexists with other models  
Source:  BNP Paribas Equity Research estimates, PhysicsX AI 
Jevons paradox 
Jevons' paradox states efficiency gains increase total consumption of a resource 
because the added efficiency lowers the effective price therefore demand expands by 
more than the efficiency saved. 
Our interactions with AI challengers suggest that the usage of incumbent platforms has 
increased as challengers make the geometry or simulation process faster and cheaper.  
Where could incremental demand land? 
– Solver consumption: a surrogate prediction still needs anchoring against a trusted 
numerical solver, as we note above this is likely to remain owned by incumbents. 
Surrogates increase the number of high-fidelity runs used as ground truth; they do not 
eliminate them. 
– PLM check-ins and data management: PLMs accumulate knowledge as more 
versions of an object are tested and become a versioned record. The data estate scales 
with iteration count and is already priced closer to consumption than to seats. Jevons 
Paradox could lower margin cost to generate more designs, and generate new insights 
for the customers.  
– Post-processing and interpretation: Human interpretation load rises with output 
volume, and that load lands in incumbent post-processing tools.  
– Physical validation and metrology: More variants reaching prototype means more 
physical correlation, inspection and reality capture. This is the least discussed of the four. 
Providers include Hexagon (PC-DMIS, coordinate measuring machines…), Zeiss (Zeiss 
Inspect, Zeiss Calypso…), Siemens (Simcenter SCADAS, NX Quality Inspection…) or 
Dassault Systemes (Delmia Inspection, 3DExp Test Data Management….).  
 


[Page 48]
 
 
BNP Paribas Research 
Software / IT Services 
16 SEPTEMBER 2026 
page 47 
Vertical software: overview 
Vertical software is built for specific industries and includes deep industry-specific 
features. Examples include: Engineering Software (Design/Manufacturing), Construction 
(Architecture Engineering Construction ‘AEC’), Professional Information, Life Sciences, 
Cybersecurity, Banking, Travel/Leisure, etc.  
Horizontal Software refers to general-purpose solutions used across industries. 
Examples include Enterprise Resource Planning ‘ERP’, Payroll, Portfolio & Project 
Management, Communication, Customer Relationship Management (‘CRM’), Human 
Capital Management (‘HCM’), Supply Chain Management (‘SCM’), IT Operations 
Management (ITOM).

Valuation on sub-sector lens  
Most resilient segments have been Cybersecurity and Engineering Software (CAD/ 
EDA), the first being perceived as necessary as AI increases cybersecurity risks and the 
latter is too complex to be disrupted.  Infrastructure Software was also broadly immune, 
positioned as necessary platforms to unify, centralise and store enterprise data before 
deploying AI. Unsurprisingly, those that endured the biggest valuation de-rating were 
SaaS apps, including Consumer/ SMB oriented apps (Intuit and Sage in accounting for 
example, or Adobe in content creation / marketing).

We also show large cap EU Software vs. other pockets of the market 
Comparing Software valuation multiples relative to themselves may be deemed futile 
given the structural AI debate now being faced, and the duration impact given change in 
bond yields since 2008-2020.  
Below we can see the extent of the relative de-rating of Software vs non-Software 
pockets of the market across Staples, Capital Goods, IT Hardware, Telecoms, Utilities. 
Software now trades at a notable discount to all end markets vs. a typical premium for 
much of the last decade, excepted IT Hardware where Software trades broadly in line. 
Semiconductor/ hardware names underwent a multiple compression recently on a 
structural re-pricing of the AI capex thesis, near-term concerns around AI safety rhetoric, 
and policy/geopolitical headwinds.

DCF Analysis: what is priced in? 
Summary: in Europe, the most heavily discounted long term operating model risks 
remain in European IT Services, pricing in negative real growth. SAP, Sage and 
Nemetschek all price in roughly half our expected growth. OVH, Temenos, Hexagon, 
Planisware all price in better growth than we expect. 
GenAI disruption fears are prompting the market to recalibrate the long-term viability of 
business models and revisit previously accepted long-term assumptions. 
We take a fresh look at a DCF analysis across our European coverage (based on BNPP 
estimates) to compare the DCF-implied intrinsic value vs. spot price. 
Secondly, we reverse engineer a simulation DCF to force the output to the current share 
prices, flexing growth assumptions only for simplicity (margin assumptions are 
unchanged): 
– In European Software/IT Services, the largest ‘growth discounts’ remain in IT 
Services with 3 of the 4 biggest discrepancies in this subsector: Alten (pricing in -11% 
growth), Sopra Steria (pricing in -7% growth) and Capgemini (pricing in 1% growth).  
– In some cases, the spot price embeds better top line growth than we currently model, 
such as: OVH, Temenos, Hexagon, Planisware, Amadeus. 
– Nemetschek has seen the biggest change since January this year, now priced for 
MSD % growth vs. double-digit % growth at the start of the year.  
– Examples where current Spot prices embed double-digit % revenue CAGR from 
2026-35 include OVHCloud (15%), Planisware (11%) and Hexagon (10%). 
Figure 82: Implied long-term growth rates in our refreshed reverse-DCF analysis

[Page 80]
 
 
BNP Paribas Research 
Software / IT Services 
16 SEPTEMBER 2026 
page 79 
 
Investment case, valuation and risks 
 
Alten (Neutral, Target Price EUR80) 
 
Investment case 
 
Alten is a play on the ER&D Services market where we see scope for a cyclical recovery 
after a downturn in key end markets such as Automotive and Manufacturing. However, 
we see less scope for sustained operating leverage given AI’s potential efficiency 
impact on outsourced Services industry economics and operating model. We prefer 
alternatives in the IT Services space with more direct exposure to customer budgets 
investing in the Enterprise AI build out. 
 
Valuation methodology 
 
We value Alten using a P/E (2028, discounted back) ~1SD below its long term median. 
 
Risks 
 
To the upside: 
 
1) the group could close more value accretive acquisitions, 2) benefit from accelerating 
demand for engineering services. 
 
To the downside: 
 
A prolonged cyclical downturn in key markets (after automotive and aerospace in the 
past few years). 
 
Dassault Systèmes (Underperform, Target Price EUR17) 
 
Investment case 
 
We see risk to DS’ mid-term targets (2029). Having already been pushed out by a year, 
we see risk of another reset given the steepening trajectory now required. We view DS 
as a mid-single digit organic growth Co. with limited scope to accelerate growth further 
or expand margins meaningfully given the bulk of the Cloud SaaS transition is still to 
materialise and platform cross-selling continues to lag. We also believe the Engineering 
Software vertical could come under increased pressure from new AI challengers and 
Frontier labs leveraging AI to disrupt the sector. In conclusion, the relative valuation 
premium to peers is unwarranted in our view. 
 
Valuation methodology 
 
We use a P/E to EPS CAGR ‘PEG’ multiple to derive our TP. P/E is CY27, EPS CAGR
is 2yr fwd, all BNPP ests inc. SBC. 
 
Risks 
 
To the upside: 
 
Risks to the upside: 1) Accelerated 3DExperience platform adoption; 2) more large deal 
wins helping drive upgrades to estimates and market sentiment ; 3) improved momentum 
in key end markets (automotive, basic resources, A&D and life sciences). 
 
To the downside: 
 
Risks to the downside: 1) slower uptake on 3DExperience platform outside of core end 
markets risks seeing DS lose market share; 2) FCF risks associated with the subscription 
transition and cash collection; 3) large M&A requiring expensive financing, limiting EPS 
accretion and adding execution risk, 4) intensifying competition from AI challengers and 
Frontier labs in the engineering software domain. 
 
Dassault Systèmes ADR (Underperform, Target Price USD20) 
 
Investment case 
 
We see risk to DS’ mid-term targets (2029). Having already been pushed out by a year, 
we see risk of another reset given the steepening trajectory now required. We view DS 
as a mid-single digit organic growth Co. with limited scope to accelerate growth further 
or expand margins meaningfully given the bulk of the Cloud SaaS transition is still to 
materialise and platform cross-selling continues to lag. We also believe the Engineering 
 


[Page 81]
 
 
 
 
BNP Paribas Research 
Software / IT Services 
16 SEPTEMBER 2026 
page 80 
Software vertical could come under increased pressure from new AI challengers and 
Frontier labs leveraging AI to disrupt the sector. In conclusion, the relative valuation 
premium to peers is unwarranted in our view. 
 
Valuation methodology 
 
We use a P/E to EPS CAGR ‘PEG’ multiple to derive our TP. P/E is CY27, EPS CAGR
is 2yr fwd, all BNPP ests inc. SBC. 
 
Risks 
 
To the upside: 
 
Risks to the upside: 1) Accelerated 3DExperience platform adoption; 2) more large deal 
wins helping drive upgrades to estimates and market sentiment ; 3) improved momentum 
in key end markets (automotive, basic resources, A&D and life sciences). 
 
To the downside: 
 
Risks to the downside: 1) slower uptake on 3DExperience platform outside of core end 
markets risks seeing DS lose market share; 2) FCF risks associated with the subscription 
transition and cash collection; 3) large M&A requiring expensive financing, limiting EPS 
accretion and adding execution risk, 4) intensifying competition from AI challengers and 
Frontier labs in the engineering software domain. 
 
Nemetschek (Outperform, Target Price EUR80) 
 
Investment case 
 
Nemetschek is a high-quality European software vendor and one of the dominant global 
vendors in the AEC market (Architecture Engineering and Construction). Having 
successfully undertaken its cloud transition since 2022 growth accelerated while 
margins were preserved, driving upgrades and a re-rating. Since then the sharp AI 
driven de-rating creates an attractive and rare valuation opportunity to own one of 
Europe's highest quality Software vendors with durable mid-teens EPS growth. 
 
Valuation methodology 
 
We value Nemetschek on a P/E multiple (2028 discounted back, BNPPE adj).  
 
Risks 
 
To the upside: 
 
We see the following upside risks: 1) revenue growth above expectations from 
international expansion in the more mature Design division, and/or from market 
penetration in the more nascent Build division leveraging Bluebeam and GoCanvas 
products; 2) internal use of GenAI could bring efficiencies in both R&D and S&M, driving 
accelerated margin expansion beyond current expectations; and 3) further accretive 
M&A.  
 
To the downside: 
 
We see the following downside risks: 1) normalising growth in the Design segment post-
cloud transition could limit upside, particularly if price increases begin to weigh on 
customer dynamics; 2) GenAI disruption could weigh on the Build (GoCanvas) division
if workflow automation / productivity tools come under pressure from Agentic AI, and the 
Media division could also come under pressure as GenAI disrupts the content creation 
ecosystem; and 3) a shift in M&A strategy could add execution risk.  
 
OVHcloud (Outperform, Target Price EUR22) 
 
Investment case 
 
OVHcloud is uniquely positioned as a European cloud Infrastructure as a Service (IaaS) 
provider, benefiting from the structural shift to cloud infrastructure, and from European 
data sovereignty regulations requiring European company data to reside and be 
processed in Europe. AI supply chain scarcity is offering OVH a growth opportunity 
thanks to the repricing of existing CPU servers and to increasing GPU hour rental 
prices. OVH could benefit from this trend shall it decide to expand its GPU server 
capacity, which would also come with headwinds on capex, FCF and EPS. Technology 
 


[Page 82]
 
 
 
 
BNP Paribas Research 
Software / IT Services 
16 SEPTEMBER 2026 
page 81 
Sovereignty, one of OVH's unique selling points since IPO, remains a limited contributor 
to group earnings and competition remains intense in Europe. We rate OVH 
Outperform. 
 
Valuation methodology 
 
We use a SoTP, valuing the Web Cloud business on 5x EV/EBITDA on '28 (non-IFRS),
and the Cloud business on steady-state revenues at €8m/MW and applying an 8x FCF 
multiple on FY30.  
 
Risks 
 
To the upside: 
 
OVH building out GPU fleet, converting into higher ASP servers. Pass through price 
increases contributing to growth above Cons estimates as churn rate is untouched. 
Sovereign cloud opportunity converting into substantial cloud contracts in Europe. Public 
Cloud growth accelerating above estimates, driven by PaaS and AI. 
 
To the downside: 
 
EPS/FCF remain negative near-term if OVH decides to build out GPU fleet, impacting 
sentiment near-term despite growth inflection story. Near-term economic cyclicality 
remains a risk for OVH given its high SMB exposure. A slowdown in the Bare metal 
segment and/or Public Cloud could impact Group revenue growth. Competitors (incl. US 
hyperscalers) obtaining SecNumCloud certification in France could further split the 
"sovereign-minded" customer base into smaller parts. 
 
SAP (Outperform, Target Price EUR230) 
 
Investment case 
 
SAP's cloud migration is in full swing and will continue to drive the growth story through 
2030. We see scope for long-term upside to Revenue growth as well as both near and 
long-term earnings and cash flow as SAP efficiently executes on this transition. We also 
see SAP as a key winner in the GenAI opportunity within Enterprise Software, having 
partnered with - and invested in - key technology providers across the technology stack. 
GenAI and Data Cloud present two exciting future growth drivers for SAP in our view. 
With the shares having de-rated in 2025 we see an opportunity for a re-rating as SAP 
proves its value in the agentic AI future.  
 
Valuation methodology 
 
We use our Software valuation framework applying a target EV/Sales and EV/EBITA
multiple to CY2028 estimates.  
 
Risks 
 
To the upside: 
 
We believe that faster than expected cloud revenue growth, margin improvements or FCF
performance would trigger upside to estimates and a re-rating of the shares.  
 
To the downside: 
 
Disappointments in execution or financial performance would likely be poorly received. 
If AI fails to materialise as an opportunity, or worse as a risk to SAP's business there is 
room for significant multiple compression. With SAP set to generate sizeable FCF, we
expect capital allocation decisions to be watched closely by the market.  
 
SAP ADR (Outperform, Target Price USD265) 
 
Investment case 
 
SAP's cloud migration is in full swing and will continue to drive the growth story through 
2030. We see scope for long-term upside to Revenue growth as well as both near and 
long-term earnings and cash flow as SAP efficiently executes on this transition. We also 
see SAP as a key winner in the GenAI opportunity within Enterprise Software, having

With the shares having de-rated in 2025 we see an opportunity for a re-rating as SAP 
proves its value in the agentic AI future.  
 
Valuation methodology 
 
We use our Software valuation framework applying a target EV/Sales and EV/EBITA
multiple to CY2028 estimates.  
 
Risks 
 
To the upside: 
 
We believe that faster than expected cloud revenue growth, margin improvements or FCF

To the downside:
